This explains why an earlier pass today's municipal-registry (WFS) search for Pterocarya near the old coordinate came back with only unrelated 1980-planted trees and no monumental match: it was querying the wrong part of the city entirely, about 500m from the square itself. Re-ran the same WFS query centered on the corrected coordinate and it resolved cleanly: among several dozen Pterocarya fraxinifolia in the square, exactly three are tagged "Monument" and share a distinct 1930 planting date (ids 1158361, 1158363, 1158364), standing apart from the surrounding 1945-and-later plantings. This matches the sources' account of "three oldest, most valuable specimens" that got underground root protection during the 2006-2010 felling dispute. Pin moved to the average position of those three trees, `location_precision` to confirmed, age corrected from the old "100+ years" placeholder to 96 years (2026 minus 1930).
- **The Cycad of the Hortus (ams_005)**: the pin was a general Hortus street address, not the Palm House itself. Found the Palmenkas building's own footprint in OpenStreetMap building data and moved the pin there (52.3663, 4.9081, about 80m from the old point). This is now a specific building, not a general garden address, so moved to confirmed; the earlier limitation (the Palm House holds more than one Encephalartos specimen, so the photo candidate still cannot be tied to this exact plant) is a photo-identity problem, not a location-honesty one, and is unchanged: photo stays `found_needs_check`.
- **Photo hunt on the Heimanseik, Vondelpark Poplar and Amstelkade Olive Willow (ams_001, ams_003, ams_008)**: re-tried Wikimedia Commons (category browsing and incategory: search combined with Dutch terms), iNaturalist's API by exact coordinate and radius, Openverse, Flickr, and bomenbieb.nl's own search. Nothing usable turned up for any of the three; this is at least the second exhaustive pass on these specific gaps today with the same result. Treating these as genuinely exhausted for now rather than retrying with the same tools again.
- Amsterdam now at 0/10 approximate pins (from 2, and one of those two was a real error rather than mere vagueness) and unchanged at 6/10 photos (1 more, the cycad, pending an identity check rather than a licence problem). Half the depth-exit-test bar for Amsterdam is now cleared; photos remain the open gap. The next pass on Amsterdam needs a genuinely new photo source (not another retry of Wikimedia/iNaturalist/Openverse/Flickr on these same three trees) or a way to view the Palm House photo directly to resolve the cycad's identity question.

## 2026-07-26 — Naples: 6 of 10 approximate pins resolved, one photo found, two discrepancies flagged for a future pass

- Naples was the worst-covered published city in the project (0/10 photos, 10/10 approximate) and had never had a depth pass since its initial research on 2026-07-25. Found and used a new source for this project: Campania's own regional monumental-tree register (`agricoltura.regione.campania.it/foreste/monum/scheda_NNN.html`), which has individual fact-sheet pages with GPS for most of Capodimonte's catalogued trees. Its photos carry no stated licence anywhere on the site, so none were usable, but the GPS and measurements were strong enough to resolve six pins by exact dimension matching (circumference and height, not just proximity).
- **Resolved to confirmed**: the Camphor of the Royal Orchard (nap_002, moved ~900m to the registry's "Canfora n.2 - Giardino Torre" point, 730cm circumference matching exactly), the Yew (nap_004, "il Tasso", 250cm/10m exact match), the Montezuma Cypress (nap_005, "Cipresso di Montezuma", 300cm circumference exact match, though its 15m registry height sits a bit under this entry's 18m, left as an open minor discrepancy), the Historic Citrus Grove (nap_006, "l'Agrumeto della Reggia di Capodimonte", 180 plants at a point consistent with nap_002's), the Villa Comunale plane (nap_009, matched via the region's own scheda already cited as a source, within 50-60m of the existing pin), and the San Severino cloister platano (nap_007, matched via the same register plus a Campania scheda specifically about this cloister, within 30m).
- **The Robust Eucalyptus (nap_003)**: the registry gave an exact circumference and height match (330cm, 30m) but placed it at "Giardino dei Principi", not "near Porta Grande" as this entry previously said. Treated the exact-measurement match as stronger evidence than the location description and corrected the address and story text accordingly, a real correction rather than added imprecision.
- **The photo found**: the San Severino platano (nap_007), via a Wikimedia Commons file whose own caption ("al centro e l'albero secolare") explicitly names the tree, not just the cloister architecture. CC BY-SA 2.0, Giuseppe Guida.
- **Two things flagged rather than resolved, worth a future pass**: (1) the Camphor of the Garden of the Princes (nap_001) has a registry candidate at Giardino dei Principi with an exact species and garden match but a circumference of 830cm against this entry's existing 670cm, a real conflict against the Capodimonte museum's own page, left unresolved rather than guessed at; pin stays approximate. (2) A research pass flagged, secondhand and unverified this round, that the Capodimonte museum's own page might identify the Robust Eucalyptus as Eucalyptus camaldulensis rather than robusta; the registry's own title explicitly says "Eucalipto robusta" so this was not acted on, but it is worth a direct check next time Naples is touched.
- **The Holm Oak of Sant'Andrea delle Dame (nap_010)** and **the Pine of Posillipo (nap_008)** stayed genuinely unresolved: the oak's cloister has registry entries for a companion camphor and a Washingtonia palm but none for the oak itself, and several Commons photos of the cloister exist but none can be confirmed (by caption or category) to show the oak specifically rather than the camphor or bare architecture; the pine, planted only in 1995, appears to be a genuine non-listing in either Italian tree registry, plausibly below their age/size threshold.
- Naples now at 3/10 approximate pins (from 10) and 1/10 photos (from 0). Site rebuilt, all contracts validated, pushed across three commits.

But the site states no reuse license anywhere, so per the hard rule these default to all-rights-reserved and are not usable, however easy the hotlink looks. Flagging clearly so a future pass does not mistake "fetchable" for "licensed."

- **Two untried leads worth a future pass**: anitagac.istanbul's individual tree detail pages (URL pattern `/agac/{ID}`) turned out to be fetchable, unlike its JS-rendered map, and contain useful facts (confirmed the Taşlı Çınar's exact site and the Ata Fıstık Çamı's name-confusion between two different pines), though the site itself carries no license statement so its own photos stay unusable. Also, `konusanagac.com`, a second İBB tree portal with per-tree pages, failed DNS resolution on every attempt this pass, a genuinely untried dead end rather than an exhausted one, and `anitagaclar.gov.tr` (the national registry) returned connection-refused rather than an empty result.
- No photo or pin changed this pass. Recorded so a future run tries the two untried leads above rather than repeating today's exhausted techniques.

- **The Inverted Ginkgo of Nishi Honganji**: found a Commons photo whose own title names this exact tree ("Grand Ginkgo Tree in West Honganji Temple"), CC BY-SA 3.0, same photographer (Soramimi) as the already-approved Land Boat Pine photo. This run's tools could not open the image file to visually confirm framing, so recorded as `found_needs_check` rather than `approved`, honest about the gap the same way earlier runs handled this same tool limitation.
- **Five-Needle Pine of Hosen-in and Camphor of Munakata Shrine**: retried both Overpass queries after the first pair timed out; both came back empty (zero tree nodes in either bounding box) rather than timing out again, so this is a real dead end for the OSM technique on these two, not just an unreachable service.
- Kyoto now at 4 of 10 approximate pins (from 6) and 5 of 10 photos (from 4). Still below both the exit-test-style bar and the 80% photo floor; left open for a future pass.

## 2026-07-25 — Verona: 33rd city live, 8 trees (not 10), 8 flagged, 6 photos missing

- New city, the next `pending` city after Bologna. Verona's documented tree heritage is genuinely thinner than several other Italian cities researched this session: only three specimens within the city itself appear on Italy's national monumental-trees register (a plane and a ginkgo group in Piazza Indipendenza, a Himalayan cedar on Viale Nino Bixio), versus eight in Bologna or six-plus in Naples's single largest park alone.
- **Deliberately shipped with 8 trees, not 10.** After a genuine search across the city's parks, gardens, cemetery and known private-estate specimens, only 8 held up to the two-source bar without stretching. Per CLAUDE.md's own mandate ("Completeness. Eight good trees ship. The ninth can come later"), chose honesty over padding rather than force two more with thin or single-source documentation.
- The headline find of this run: the Cedar of Lebanon in Piazza Bra, right beside the Arena, made real local news in mid-2025 when its roots showed signs of failure and the city raced to stabilise it before Ferragosto, tracked in real time across five-plus independent Veronese outlets. Planted 1873, one of the last survivors of the square's original garden design.
- One honesty call worth flagging directly: the famous Cypress of Goethe in the Giardino Giusti, over 600 years old and the garden's single most celebrated feature, was uprooted by a storm on 23 August 2020 and no longer exists. The garden's cypress avenue entry describes what stands there today (younger replanted cypresses alongside surviving older ones the storm did not reach), explicitly not claiming Goethe's own tree is still standing. A set of pre-storm 1993-94 documentation photos exists on Commons but was deliberately not used, since it would misrepresent the avenue's current state, the same discipline applied to Naples's Pino di Posillipo.
- One single-sourced entry kept rather than dropped: the Himalayan cedar of Villa Bersani, a beloved private-property landmark that residents petitioned (unsuccessfully so far) to add to the monumental register in 2015. Only one dedicated source found despite a genuine search; kept because the underlying claim (a real petition, a real tree at a real street corner) is specific and plausible rather than vague. Access honestly marked as street-visible only, since the tree stands on private grounds.
- 2 of 10 photos found, both Wikimedia Commons, both CC BY-SA 4.0, both fetched and viewed directly before approval: the ginkgo and the plane tree of Piazza Indipendenza. **6 missing** after a genuine search across Commons and iNaturalist for the rest, including the Piazza Bra cedar itself, which despite the square's own well-photographed Commons category had no dedicated tree-focused shot found.
- Site rebuilt, all contracts validated on the first pass, pushed.

- New city, the next `pending` city after Naples. Researched via Italy's national monumental-trees register (Alberi Monumentali d'Italia, RAMI), the Emilia-Romagna region's own parallel catalogue, and several dedicated Bologna tree-history blogs and press features. Eight of Italy's officially monumental trees stand within Bologna city itself; this list uses most of them, filled out with two further well-documented specimens (a hospital-garden cedar, a private-estate cedar) from outside that specific register.

Neither was used; both are noted directly in the relevant tree's own curation notes so a future run does not re-walk the same dead end.

Caught on review and replaced with the correct, Bologna-specific source before commit.
- **10 trees flagged, all honestly, none fabricated**: two entries (the Montagnola planes, the San Michele in Bosco camphor) are presented as ensembles or general-presence entries rather than one dated specimen, since neither source set names one individual tree with its own measurements; the Piazza Minghetti tree's "tallest monumental tree in the province" claim is presented as sourced rather than independently checked against a full provincial register.

## 2026-07-25 — Naples: 31st city live, 10 trees, 10 flagged, 10 photos missing

- New city, the next `pending` city after Granada. Naples tops Italy's own official national register of monumental trees, more catalogued specimens than any other municipality, and most of that count concentrates in one place: the Real Bosco di Capodimonte, the former Bourbon royal hunting estate, whose own museum has an official page naming six specifically monumental trees. Six of this city's ten trees come from that single collection, a deliberate choice rather than laziness: that really is where Naples kept its trees, and the intro says so directly rather than pretending otherwise.
- Two honesty calls worth flagging clearly. First, the Pino di Posillipo: the original stone pine, one of the most photographed trees in the world through Naples's postcard era, died in 1984 and was replanted by Legambiente in 1995. The tree included here is explicitly labelled a young replacement, the same standard applied to Edinburgh's Corstorphine sycamore, and historical Wikimedia Commons photographs of the original, long-dead tree were deliberately not used since they would misrepresent what a visitor sees today. Second, the Platano di San Severino: felled in 1959 after termite damage (documented at 8.45m circumference), regrown from its own root rather than replanted from scratch, so its age here is calculated from the 1959 regrowth rather than the legendary original planting attributed to Saint Benedict, which is presented explicitly as legend, not fact.
- One thing considered and rejected: the Orto Botanico di Napoli's own "ficus magnolioide" turned up repeatedly in search results almost entirely conflated with Palermo's far more famous specimen of the same common name (a different tree, in a different pending city on this project's own list). Rather than risk publishing a wrong-city mixup, dropped it in favour of a better-sourced Naples specimen (the Sant'Andrea delle Dame holm oak).
- **10 trees flagged, all honestly, none fabricated**: the citrus grove is presented as a collective 2021-listed planting rather than one dated tree, the same honest framing used for other cities' avenue/grove entries; several Capodimonte ages are given as broad 19th century ranges since the park's own official page gives dimensions but not exact planting years for most specimens.
- **0 of 10 photos found** despite a genuine search across Wikimedia Commons and iNaturalist for every tree, including targeted category browsing of Capodimonte, Villa Comunale and Posillipo. All 10 recorded honestly as missing rather than stretched; a strong candidate existed for the Posillipo pine (historical photos of the pre-1984 original) but was deliberately not used per the honesty note above.
- Site rebuilt, all contracts validated on the first pass, pushed.

## 2026-07-25 — Granada: 30th city live, 10 trees, 10 flagged, 9 photos missing

- New city, the next `pending` city after Malaga. Granada has no single municipal tree register; researched via a magazine survey of twelve singular trees across the city (wastemagazine.es), the Alhambra and Generalife's own official heritage pages, and the University of Granada Botanical Garden's own records.
- A real discrepancy caught and resolved rather than picked from convenience: the popular San Juan de la Cruz cypress story (planted 1582-1588 at Carmen de los Martires, tied to a documented six-year residency) rounds to "500 years" in several tourism sources, but the Alhambra Patronato's own official page describes a similarly named "Cedro de San Juan" at a different property, Carmen de Bellavista, with its own specific measurements and a note of wind damage and decline. Rather than merge the two or default to the more dramatic figure, used the more conservative, better-documented age (roughly 440 years, calculated from the specific planting years) and kept the two possibly-different trees distinct rather than conflated, flagging the discrepancy directly in the entry's notes for a future run to resolve if a clearer source turns up.
- Two trees considered and dropped rather than published: a nettle tree at the Alhambra's Abencerrajes Palace and the original "Cipres de la Sultana" at the Generalife, both confirmed dead (2020 and the late 1980s respectively, the latter's dry trunk preserved behind glass with a plaque but not a living tree). A city page for a species that no longer exists to visit fails the entire premise of this project, so both were excluded rather than published with a caveat.
- One access case handled the same honest way as Malaga's school avocado: Carmen de la Victoria's cedars stand on University of Granada residence grounds, free to enter but only by prior appointment through the university's own booking system, and the access field says exactly that.
- **10 trees flagged, all honestly, none fabricated**: two entries (the Parador's twin cypresses and its sophora) are sourced to two articles from the same publisher rather than fully independent outlets, flagged as closer to single-sourced despite technically citing two URLs; the Archbishop's Palace palms rest on one dedicated source with no second one found; the robinia's own source literally states a "4.60 metre diameter" trunk, physically implausible for the species, silently reinterpreted here as girth/circumference (the measurement convention actually consistent with the number) rather than repeated as written.
- 1 of 10 photos found, via iNaturalist rather than Wikimedia Commons: the robinia of Cuarto Real de Santo Domingo, CC BY, fetched and viewed directly, showing the tree's distinctive thick, gnarled trunk in bloom, coordinates 90m from the entrance address. **9 missing** after a genuine search across Commons and iNaturalist for the rest, including several close misses opened and rejected (the Ceiba/robinia-adjacent candidates aside, no Commons category for the Alhambra forest, the Generalife's Jardines Altos, or the Ginkgo biloba in Spain category yielded a confirmed match for this city's specific specimens).
- Site rebuilt, all contracts validated (one meta description trimmed to fit the 155-character limit), pushed.

## 2026-07-25 — Malaga: 29th city live, 10 trees, 10 flagged, 6 photos missing

- New city, the next `pending` city after the photo-floor rung was checked and found exhausted for the existing 28 (see entry below). Researched via Malaga's 2022 municipal TreeTags scheme (part of the European TreeTags project and the Tree Cities of the World programme, ten trees tagged citywide for size, age or location, corroborated by two independent press outlets covering the same campaign) and the Jardin Botanico-Historico La Concepcion's own "historic plants" pages (a walled botanical garden open since 1855, declared a historic garden in 1943).
- The throughline that fell out of the research: almost nothing on this list is native. Ficus from Australia and India, ceibas from South America, an araucaria from a single Pacific island, an avocado from the Americas, all planted through 19th and 20th century fashion and trade. The one Andalusian native, a holm oak in Parque de Malaga, made the cut specifically for being the exception, used as the honest throughline in both the intro and one tree's own story rather than smoothed over.
- **10 trees flagged, all honestly, none fabricated**: the Concepcion olive's "400 years, moved from Velez-Malaga" figure ultimately traces to the garden's own page rather than a fully independent second source; six of the ten TreeTags-sourced trees are corroborated by two press outlets that both appear to draw on the same underlying municipal campaign, so treated as closer to single-sourced for their specific figures and flagged as such rather than counted as fully independent; two superlative claims (the Barrilito's "possibly thickest of its kind in Spain/Europe", the Concepcion araucaria's "tallest in the province") are both kept with the source's own hedge intact per hard rule 8.
- One access note handled carefully per the mandate: the Churriana avocado tree stands on a working primary school's grounds, not freely open to the public. Kept rather than dropped, since the school's own information confirms visits are possible by prior appointment, and the access field says exactly that rather than implying free public entry. This is a "how open is it, honestly described" case, not a hard-rule-10 withheld-location case; the location itself is precisely known and publicly stated by the school and the city's own campaign.
- 3 of 10 photos found, all Wikimedia Commons, all CC BY-SA 4.0 or CC BY 2.0, every one fetched and viewed directly before approval: the Concepcion olive (its own named plaza, "Plaza del Olivo Centenario", an exact filename and visual match), the Alameda Principal's ficus avenue (a street-level shot clearly showing the canopy), and El Barrilito (a bare-winter shot showing the distinctive swollen trunk and the red urban-art benches described in press coverage). **6 missing** after a genuine search across Commons and iNaturalist for the rest: the Cortina del Muelle ceiba (the street's own 16-file Commons category covers only buildings and plaques), the Picasso Gardens ficus (its 8-file Commons category covers a sister-city sculpture and a different tree's pollen cones, not this one), the Concepcion hackberry and araucaria (both well documented in text, no photo found for either specific specimen), the Parque de Malaga holm oak and cañafístula (TreeTags-tagged but undocumented in image form), and the Churriana avocado (visible only through the school's own social media, not an open licence).
- Site rebuilt, all contracts validated (two meta descriptions initially ran over the 155-character limit and were shortened), pushed.

Replaced with the Kashmir cypress of the university's Jardí Botanic, a genuinely rare, currently living species.

One tree's most colourful detail, a councilwoman physically blocking excavators to save the Avenida de Aragón holm oak, appears in only one of the two sources checked, so it is noted in this entry's internal notes but not stated as confirmed fact in the story text.

## 2026-07-25 — Lyon: 27th city live, 10 trees, 10 flagged, 9 photos missing

- New city, the next `pending` in the tier list after Nice. No reader submissions (CSV checked fresh: header row only). Site healthy, nothing published was wrong.
- Lyon was the best-documented city yet: the city government publishes an actual list of nationally labelled Arbres Remarquables (four individual trees, plus Parc de la Tête d'Or's own "Ensemble Arboré Remarquable" label for its full 8,800-tree collection), and a dendrology blog (lestetardsarboricoles.fr) plus the International Dendrology Society's own reference site (treesandshrubsonline.org) gave precise GPS coordinates, measurements and even a "oldest cultivated specimen in Europe" claim for individual Tête d'Or trees, letting 3 of the 10 entries carry `location_precision: confirmed` rather than approximate, more than any other city on the site so far.
- The throughline, used as the city's own intro: Lyon's remarkable trees split into a formally documented arboretum (Tête d'Or, built from 1856 by Swiss landscape architects, absorbing the city's old botanical garden in 1857) and a handful of individually labelled specimens scattered across quieter neighbourhood parks, plus one genuine outlier, a cedar in the garden of a Saône island abbey with roots going back to the 4th century.
- **A genuine age dispute caught and named rather than silently resolved**: the Atlas cedar at Parc de la Chapelle carries a locally repeated claim of roughly 500 years, officially labelled as a national remarkable tree on 16 October 2024 with real, independently confirmed measurements (28m tall, 32m crown, 6.35m girth). But Cedrus atlantica was not introduced into French cultivation until the 1840s, which makes a genuine 500-year-old specimen hard to reconcile with the species' own documented history in Europe. The claim is stated as a claim, not adopted as settled fact, per hard rule 2 and P7.
- All 10 flagged honestly. None fabricated. 3 of 10 presented as explicit ensembles (Parc de la Cerisaie's 1913 oak collection, Jardin des Chartreux's undated garden trees, and initially a third that was swapped for a stronger measured specimen, the Cappadocian Maple, once found) rather than forcing a single named specimen where none is documented.
- 1 of 10 photos found on Wikimedia Commons (the Tête d'Or's Osage orange, CC BY 4.0, filename and description both confirming species and exact park), marked `found_needs_check` rather than `approved` for the same tool-constraint reason as Venice, Munich and Nice this run: images could not be opened and visually confirmed this session. One near-miss caught and correctly rejected: a matching Flickr photo of the Pin de Bunge was found but marked All Rights Reserved, not a usable licence, so not used per hard rule 4. 9 of 10 photos missing after a genuine search.
- Site rebuilt, all contracts validated, pushed.

## 2026-07-25 — Nice: 26th city live, 10 trees, 10 flagged, 8 photos missing

- New city, the next `pending` in the tier list after Munich. No reader submissions (CSV checked fresh: header row only). Site healthy, nothing published was wrong.
- Researched via piedsdazur.fr, provence7.com, the regional Inventaire Général du Patrimoine Culturel (maregionsud.fr), nice.fr, culture.gouv.fr's Jardin Remarquable label pages, and ANNAM's dedicated Alpes-Maritimes remarkable-trees registry (asnatnic.fr).
- The throughline, used as the city's own intro: almost everything remarkable about Nice's trees is recent and deliberate, a public garden planted in 1851, a promenade planted with palms from 1862, a whole hillside forest replanted by hand the same decade after being stripped bare, because Nice as a resort city is itself a mid-19th-century creation. The one real exception is Cimiez, gardened continuously since Franciscan monks arrived in 1546, whose olive grove holds this city's only trees plausibly over 400 years old.
- One private-land exclusion caught and applied per hard rule 10: two well-documented ~500-year-old carob trees at a private Nice property (labelled "arbre remarquable" by a regional association) were dropped after a direct check found the trees explicitly "not open for visits." A different labelled specimen, an Arbutus unedo at Château de Crémat, a working Bellet vineyard estate with genuine booked public tours, was used instead, the only entry in this city with a precisely surveyed coordinate (from the label registry itself) rather than an approximate pin.
- All 10 flagged honestly, none fabricated. 6 of the 10 are presented as explicit ensembles (a garden's founding-era planting or a documented reforestation campaign) rather than a single named specimen, since no individual tree in any of them has a separately measured age, consistent with how this project has handled undated groves elsewhere (Antwerp's Den Brandt, Athens's cemetery cypresses). One softened superlative per hard rule 8: Parc Vigier's "first Phoenix canariensis planted in France, 1864" claim rests on a single detailed source with no independent corroboration found, so the story attributes it as a claim rather than stating it as settled fact.
- One human-interest find worth naming: Henri Matisse is buried under an old olive tree on land adjoining the Cimetière du Monastère de Cimiez, a short walk from the Musée Matisse, freely visitable.
- 2 of 10 photos found on Wikimedia Commons with filename/category matches (the Cimiez monastery garden, the Cimetière du Château), both marked `found_needs_check` rather than `approved` for the same reason as Venice and Munich this run: this session's tools could not open image files to visually confirm content. 8 of 10 photos missing after a genuine search.
- Site rebuilt, all contracts validated, pushed.

## 2026-07-25 — Munich: 25th city live, 10 trees, 10 flagged, 5 photos missing

- New city, the next `pending` in the tier list after Venice. No reader submissions (CSV checked fresh: header row only). Site healthy, nothing published was wrong.
- Munich turned out to be unusually well documented: a city ordinance renewed 21 July 2021 individually registers 117 Naturdenkmäler covering 200 trees, each with an official rationale (age, rarity, size or historical association), and German Wikipedia's own list of that ordinance gave a strong starting point cross-checked against baumkunde.de (a German dendrology registry), Wikimedia Commons categories, and local press.
- **Genuine, unresolved "oldest tree in Munich" conflict, presented honestly rather than picked**: a Sendling-Westpark district citizen assembly document dates the Krüner Eiche oak at roughly 600 years, while a dedicated local news feature separately calls the Röth-Linde (300-350 years) the city's oldest tree. Both claims are sourced, neither has been reconciled against the other, and both flagged accordingly rather than silently choosing one per hard rule 8.
- All 10 trees flagged honestly. None fabricated. Most ages come from the city's own register as broad, undated estimates ("over 300 years", "extremely rare species", "unique in size") rather than individual measurements, which is stated directly in each entry rather than dressed up as more precise than it is. One exception: the Reichs- und Friedenseiche has an exact, plaque-documented planting date of 1 July 1871, planted by 12,000 Munich schoolchildren after the Franco-Prussian War, one of the more precisely dated trees on the whole site.
- 5 of 10 photos found on Wikimedia Commons with matching filenames/categories (Friedenseiche, the twin Flatterulmen, the fern-leaved beech, the Karolinenplatz pagoda tree, the Maximiliansplatz wych elm), all CC BY-SA. **All 5 marked `found_needs_check` rather than `approved`**: this run's tools could not fetch or open image files to visually confirm content (see the Venice entry above for the same constraint), so textual matching (exact filenames, Naturdenkmal category names) was the strongest verification available. One real catch from that check: an initial URL guess for each photo (built by hand-guessing Wikimedia's hash-path convention) was wrong for every single file and would have shipped as five dead links; recomputed correctly via MD5 of the actual filename and verified against a known-good existing entry (Milan's bagolaro photo) before use. 5 of 10 photos missing after a genuine search (Röth-Linde, Krüner Eiche, the Englischer Garten beech, the Nymphenburg oak, the Oskar-von-Miller-Ring hackberries).

## 2026-07-25 — Venice: 24th city live, 10 trees, 10 flagged, 6 photos missing

- New city, the next `pending` in the tier list after Milan. No reader submissions to process first (CSV checked: header row only, fetched fresh). The photo-floor rung was checked and found exhausted (every live city already had a genuine dedicated hunt in the 2026-07-22/23/24 sweep), so went straight to the next pending city per the ladder.
- Researched via Italian and English Wikipedia, the Comune di Venezia's own pages, italianbotanicalheritage.com, and a range of local history sources (conoscerevenezia.it, evenice.it, veneziaunica.it) for the city's gardens and lagoon islands. Comune di Venezia's actual administrative territory, the historic islands, the Lido, and mainland Mestre/Marghera, was used as the boundary, matching the "Greater London" approach used for other sprawling cities.
- The throughline, used as the city's own intro: Venice's historic core has almost no open ground for a tree to grow old by accident, so every tree on this list stands in a garden, cemetery or park someone deliberately created, mostly from the 18th century onward (San Lazzaro degli Armeni's monastery garden, 1717) or the Napoleonic period (the Biennale Gardens, 1810; San Michele cemetery, 1813).
- **All 10 trees flagged, all honestly, none fabricated.** This is a harder city than most for individual tree ages: no source gives any single Venice tree its own measured age or planting date, only a founding or construction date for the garden, cemetery or fortress around it. Every entry states that gap directly rather than borrowing the site's founding date as the tree's own age, and 6 of the 10 are presented as explicit ensembles (a species mix or a documented planted line, following the precedent set by Antwerp's Den Brandt and Athens's cemetery cypresses) rather than forcing a single named specimen where none is documented.
- Access notes worth flagging: San Lazzaro degli Armeni is paid (8 euro, one guided tour daily at 15:25) and San Francesco del Deserto requires a private boat from Burano (no public vaporetto stop), both genuinely open to visitors but not a casual walk-in. Ca' Zenobio degli Armeni's garden is marked honestly as open mainly during scheduled exhibitions rather than year-round.

One photo (Giardini Papadopoli) was kept on strong textual evidence, coordinates matching to the metre and a filename that translates as "trees", but marked `found_needs_check` rather than `approved` since it was never actually opened. Three further candidates with plausible but unconfirmed content (San Francesco del Deserto, San Servolo, Ca' Zenobio) are logged in each tree's `notes` field with the exact filename, so a future run with working image tools can finish the check quickly. **6 of 10 photos missing** after a genuine search across Wikimedia Commons.
- Site rebuilt, all contracts validated, pushed.

## 2026-07-24 — Milan: 23rd city live, 10 trees, 6 flagged, 5 photos missing

- New city, the next `pending` in the tier list after Florence. No reader submissions to process first (CSV checked: header row only, fetched fresh). Researched via Italy's national/regional monumental tree registry (RAMI, ilregistrodeglialberi.it, which covers 113 individually registered Milan trees with GPS and measurements for most), Wikidata, the Comune di Milano's own monumental-tree pages, Orti Botanici Unimi (Orto Botanico di Brera), local history blogs (milanoaffori.it, Urbanfile, milanocittastato.it, studiohomoradix.com), and a restoration cooperative's own adoption records (demetra.net). monumentaltrees.com was not used at all this run (no Milan page was needed for verification, since RAMI's own registry already gave GPS and measurements directly).
- The throughline that fell out of the research, used as the city's own intro: Milan has almost nothing medieval standing, having rebuilt itself too many times for old wood to survive by accident. What lasted did so because of a specific human decision: a university kept planting records (the Orto Botanico di Brera's 1775 ginkgo pair), a neighbourhood organised (Brera's paulownia, saved from a car park by a resident committee in 2016), or a park changed its function entirely (Parco Trotter, a horse-racing oval turned into an open-air school for sick children in 1919, still a working school today).
- **6 of 10 trees flagged, all honestly, none fabricated**:
  - The Orto Botanico's ginkgo pair (mil_004): two calculation methods disagree by 16 years (RAMI's 235-year growth-data estimate vs. the garden's own 250th-anniversary framing implying 251 years from the 1775 planting record). Presented as a 235-251 range.
  - The Affori plane, La Pianta (mil_009): a genuine, checkable dispute between a 2013 dendrochronological core sample (about 187 years) and the popular Napoleonic-era legend (over 250 years, tied to a story about Napoleon and a Milanese noblewoman). Presented as a range with the scientific figure given more weight, per hard rule 8/P7, rather than repeating the legend as fact.
  - The Giardini Montanelli plane (mil_002), Guastalla catalpa (mil_005), Giardini Montanelli hackberry (mil_003) and Parco Sempione walnut (mil_010): each has a real location and species confirmed by two sources, but the specific age figure (where any exists at all) traces to RAMI's registry alone rather than a second independent source, or no age was ever documented and a wide honest range is given instead.
  - The Brera paulownia (mil_008) is flagged separately: it has no RAMI registry entry at all (it is a self-seeded tree on a WWII bomb site, never deliberately planted), so its "over 65 years" age comes from press reporting rather than any measurement.
- **One superlative caught and corrected**: local press repeatedly calls the red oak at Piazza XXIV Maggio (mil_006) "the oldest tree in Milan." Checked against this list's own ginkgo pair (235-251 years, well documented) and other candidates, and explicitly rejected in the tree's own story text per hard rule 8, since the oak is only about 131 years old. The oak's real story, planted in 1924 by a father in memory of his son and the other Alpini who died in the First World War, is corroborated by three independent sources and used as the lead fact instead.
- **Two corrections caught mid-research, both prevented from shipping wrong rather than just vague**:
  - The Quercia di Montale, a roughly 200-year-old red oak once popularly linked to the poet Eugenio Montale, fell in October 2019 during heavy rain. Its trunk is preserved in place as a deliberate biodiversity exhibit rather than removed, but it is not a living tree to visit, so it was dropped from the list of 10 and named instead, honestly, in the city's own FAQ.
  - A widely repeated candidate, "the 1773 plane tree of Villa Litta" in Affori, turned out on checking to have been felled in 2015 after a fungal disease; only a preserved cross-section ("el Rondelun") survives on display inside the villa. Dropped entirely and replaced on the list with a different, still-living, well-documented plane in the same neighbourhood: La Pianta at the corner of Viale Affori and Via Astesani.
- **One tree dropped for thin sourcing, not hard rule 10**: an initial candidate, a cedar of Lebanon at Largo Rio de Janeiro (Città Studi), is individually registered by RAMI but has zero measurements, no age, and no second independent source describing it at all, not even a mention in general neighbourhood history writeups. Replaced with a considerably better-documented alternative in the same slot: the red oak of Parco Trotter, a former horse-racing track turned 1919 open-air school, which has real history, two independent sources and a confirmed GPS point.
- No hard-rule-10 exclusions this run: every one of the 10 published trees stands on public, visitor-open land (public parks, a free university botanical garden, public squares, and a public street corner), and Parco Trotter's restricted-to-school-hours access is noted plainly in its access field rather than hidden.
- **5 of 10 photos found**, all Wikimedia Commons, all CC BY-SA 3.0 or 4.0, every one fetched as a raw image and viewed directly before approval, not caption-matched: the Guastalla catalpa in bloom (mil_005), the Parco Sempione walnut sprawling over its pond (mil_010, geotagged within 70m of the registry's own GPS point), the Giardini Montanelli hackberry (mil_003), the Piazza XXIV Maggio red oak at its traffic-circle setting (mil_006), and the gnarled trunk of La Pianta at Affori (mil_009, whose own caption names it explicitly). **5 missing** after a genuine search: a generic "centuries-old plane" photo in Giardini Montanelli (mil_002) was viewed and rejected since the park holds several large planes and the caption does not identify which one; two Commons botanical-garden photos were viewed and rejected for the ginkgo (mil_004) since neither identifies a ginkgo specifically; two Flickr photos of the exact Brera paulownia (mil_008) were found but are both marked All Rights Reserved; and no photography at all turned up for the Giardini Montanelli cypress (mil_001) or the Parco Trotter oak (mil_007).
- Species note: two Northern Red Oaks (Quercus rubra) and two London Planes (Platanus x hispanica) appear on this list, in different parks with different, independently sourced stories (a WWI memorial vs. a former school; a park specimen vs. a suburban street-corner legend), kept separate rather than merged, matching how other cities on this site have handled repeated species.
- Site rebuilt, all contracts validated on the first pass, pushed.

## 2026-07-24 — Florence: 22nd city live, 10 trees, 8 flagged, 8 photos missing

- New city, the next `pending` in the tier-1 list after Athens. Researched via Italy's national/regional monumental tree registry (RAMI, ilregistrodeglialberi.it, which gives per-tree GPS coordinates and measurements for 145 individually registered Florence trees), the Comune di Firenze's own 2026 press materials on its 19-candidate/6-already-recognised monumental tree program, ascuoladaglialberi.net's Tuscany survey, and English/Italian Wikipedia. monumentaltrees.com was checked for verification only, never copied, per hard rule 1.
- The throughline that fell out of the research, used as the city's own intro: Florence's oldest documented trees split cleanly into two eras. The Giardino dei Semplici, a botanical garden founded in 1545 to grow medicine for the Medici, holds the city's only trees with genuinely documented planting dates (a yew from 1720, a cork oak from 1805), because its directors kept their own records. Almost everything else on the list dates from a single decade, the 1860s-70s, when Florence briefly served as capital of the newly unified Kingdom of Italy and architect Giuseppe Poggi laid out new parks, boulevards and viewpoints (Piazzale Michelangelo, the Bobolino) across the hills above the Arno.
- **8 of 10 trees flagged, all honestly, none fabricated**: most for undocumented age (the Cascine's farnia and leccio, the Salviatino honey locust, the Bobolino and Boboli cedars, the Piazzale Michelangelo ginkgo, the Villa Vogel pine all carry wide, honest age ranges rather than a false precise figure, since RAMI records girth and location meticulously but frequently not age). One genuine age dispute: the Montezuma cypress at the Giardino dei Semplici, where RAMI's registry gives a specific 1884 planting date (about 142 years) while an independent Tuscany monumental-tree survey (ascuoladaglialberi.net) gives 115 years from an earlier assessment; presented as a 115-145 year range rather than picking either figure, per hard rule 8/P7.
- **Two real corrections caught mid-research, both prevented from shipping wrong**:
  - An initial candidate, the "Albero della Pace" cedar of Villa Fabbricotti (a much-repeated local landmark, once claimed as one of the city's finest cedars), turned out on checking to have died in 2001; only a bare trunk remains. Dropped entirely rather than published as a living tree to visit.
  - A second initial candidate, the officially-recognised monumental elm of Piazza Vittorio Veneto (repeatedly described in older press and tourism sources as "notably long-lived for an elm"), turned out via RAMI's own record to have died and been felled in July 2023 after years of decline. Dropped entirely; this would have been a classic "wrong, not just vague" pin had it shipped from older secondary sources alone.
- **One hard-rule-10 exclusion**: the Chilean palm of Villa di Rusciano, a regionally-registered monumental tree, is explicitly marked "not accessible to the public" in its own RAMI registry entry, directly contradicting looser secondary sources describing the surrounding park as public since the 1990s. Given the direct conflict, and since the palm itself (rather than just the surrounding grounds) is the specific thing in question, left off the list per "when in doubt, leave it out."
- **Other candidates researched then dropped, not on hard rule 10 but for weaker sourcing or ambiguity**: the wisteria pergola of Villa Bardini (visually spectacular and popular, but no verifiable planting date for the vines themselves beyond a 2005 pergola restoration, so no honest age could be given); the oak of Mantignano (location within the neighbourhood could not be pinned precisely enough to separate it from an unrelated, newly-opened adjacent park); the cedar of Villa Favard (its park now doubles as a conservatory's grounds and current public-access policy could not be confirmed); San Salvi's bagolari and pine (no individually registered entry with coordinates found, and the area's trees suffered a documented 2018 tornado with unclear survivorship); the holm oak of Via del Monte alle Croci (single-sourced only).
- One important naming clarification worth flagging: Florence has two unrelated trees popularly associated with Judas in Italian. "Albero di Giuda" (Cercis siliquastrum, the pink-flowered Judas Tree) is not on this list; the honey locust included here is "Spino di Giuda", a different species named for its thorns. Spelled out explicitly in the tree's own story to avoid the mix-up.
- **2 of 10 photos found**, both Wikimedia Commons, both fetched and described directly before approval rather than caption-matched: the Montezuma cypress at the Giardino dei Semplici (geotagged to the garden itself, CC BY-SA 3.0) and the ginkgo at Piazzale Michelangelo (explicitly captioned for that location, CC BY-SA 4.0). **8 missing** after a genuine search across Wikimedia Commons (including targeted category browsing of the Giardino dei Semplici's 416-file category and the Cascine's category), search-engine image queries, and iNaturalist/Openverse-style queries for each remaining tree; none of Florence's RAMI registry photos were used since the registry's own licensing terms for its citizen-contributed photos are unclear, not a verified open licence.
- All 10 pins are GPS coordinates taken from RAMI's own per-tree survey points (an independent, dedicated monumental-tree registry), which is why every tree here is marked `location_precision: confirmed` rather than approximate, a stronger position than several earlier cities on this list.
- Site rebuilt, all contracts validated on the first pass, pushed.

## 2026-07-24 — Athens: 21st city live, 10 trees, 10 flagged, 6 photos missing

- New city, the next `pending` in the tier-1 list after Porto. Researched via English and Greek-language search (Athens Voice, LiFO, dasarxeio.com, ypaithros.gr), Wikipedia EN/EL, Atlas Obscura, monumentaltrees.com (verification only), and dedicated pages for individual sites (RBGE-style institutional pages don't exist here, so National Garden/Zappeion/First Cemetery history came from the European Garden Heritage Network, zappeion.gr and Wikipedia respectively).
- The throughline: Athens has been burned, besieged and rebuilt too many times in three millennia for medieval-aged trees to survive in the open. What the city has instead is continuity (an olive on the spot Athena is mythologised to have grown the first one, another regrown from Plato's Academy's root system after a 1976 bus accident) or deliberate, well-documented planting (the National Garden and Zappeion, both 19th century; the Filopappou Hill olive woodland, a celebrated 1950s landscape-architecture project). Used honestly as the city's own intro rather than forcing a false "ancient city" framing to match other pages.
- **10 trees flagged, all honestly, none fabricated.** The pattern here differs from most cities: almost every entry carries some form of age uncertainty, because Athens' candidates split between mythologised continuity (hard to date the "real" lineage) and ensemble plantings (no individual specimen has a published measurement). Specific flags:
  - **The Sacred Olive Tree of the Acropolis**: sources disagree on when the current physical tree was replanted (1917 vs. 1952, after WWII German damage), presented as a range. Age fields describe the actual standing wood (70-110 years), not the mythologised 2,500-year lineage to 480 BC, which is told as legend in the story text.
  - **The Olive Tree of Pisistratus**: the "2,500+ years, oldest olive on Earth" claim traces to a single 1919 Hellenic Archaeological Society attestation, not a modern radiocarbon or dendrochronological study. Checked against the broader science: radiocarbon studies of comparably old Mediterranean olives generally find real ages in the low hundreds once tested, since olive trunks rot hollow from the centre and take their earliest rings with them. Softened per hard rule 8: age given as a 300-2,500 year range spanning both figures rather than asserting either, and the "oldest on Earth" superlative dropped.
  - **The Regrown Olive of Plato's Academy**: labelled "Young regrowth" (under 50 years, since the 1976 bus accident that split the original trunk), same honesty pattern as Edinburgh's Corstorphine Sycamore and Antwerp's Peace Tree. The claim that the Academy's grove itself descended from Acropolis-olive cuttings is stated as tradition, not verified fact.
  - **The Transplanted Olive of Megali tou Genous Scholis**: age (up to 1,500 years) traces to an Agricultural University of Athens assessment cited across multiple Greek press outlets covering ERGOSE's olive-rescue program, but the program moved trees ranging 700-1,500 years to several different Athens sites, and no single source gives this specific tree its own individually confirmed figure separate from the program's general range. Given as a 700-1,500 year range rather than asserting the higher figure alone.
  - **The Cypresses of the First Cemetery**, **the Holm Oak of the National Garden**, **the Plane Trees of Zappeion**, **the Plane Trees of Kefalari**, and **the Trees of Kaisariani Monastery**: all ensemble or single-representative entries where the institution's own founding date is well documented but no individual tree has a published age or measurement; ranges are honest estimates rather than register figures, stated as such in each entry's notes.
  - **The Olive Woodland of Filopappou Hill**: corrected mid-research (see below), presented as a deliberately young (~70 year) planting rather than an ancient grove.
- **One real correction caught mid-research**: an initial lead described Filopappou Hill's olive cover as centuries-old, gnarled trees, "the kind that take on character with age". Checking further, two independent architecture-history sources (documenta14.de, tarn.studio) instead date the entire olive woodland to architect Dimitris Pikionis's 1954-1957 landscape redesign of the hill, which replaced a mostly bare, heavily-grazed slope with hundreds of newly planted olives as part of what is now considered one of the twentieth century's most significant works of landscape architecture. Rewrote the entry around the true age and the far more interesting design story, rather than the invented antiquity.
- **One entry replaced entirely during photo-hunting**: the initial fourth tree, a generic pair of undated Acropolis cypresses included mainly to round out the list, was dropped and replaced with a considerably stronger find: a specific, precisely located olive tree that Greece's rail construction authority ERGOSE transplanted whole, roots and all, from a village near Aigio to a traffic island on Vasilissis Sofias Avenue in December 2015, to save it from a railway project's expropriation zone. Better sourced (5+ independent Greek and English press outlets, an Agricultural University age assessment, an ERGOSE press release) and better photographed than what it replaced.
- **4 of 10 photos found**, all Wikimedia Commons, every one fetched as a raw image and viewed directly before approval, not caption-matched: **ath_001** (the Acropolis olive against the Erechtheion wall, CC BY-SA 2.0), **ath_004** (the ERGOSE-transplanted olive with its explanatory plaque, CC BY-SA 4.0), **ath_005** (the National Garden holm oak, CC BY 3.0), **ath_006** (the First Cemetery entrance framed by cypresses, CC BY 3.0, an ensemble shot rather than a single-tree close-up, the same bar used for other ensemble entries on this list). **6 missing** after a genuine search across Commons, iNaturalist, Openverse and general web search for each: the Pisistratus olive (Agioi Anargyroi is thinly photographed on Commons generally), Plato's Academy's regrowth, the Zappeion's plane avenue (its Commons category is almost entirely statues and architectural detail, no tree-focused files), Filopappou's olive woodland, Kaisariani's courtyard trees, and Kefalari's planes (one promising candidate, a Commons "Quality Image" of Kefalari Park, was opened and rejected: its dominant trees read as large conifers/cedars rather than the Platanus orientalis this entry describes, and using it would risk the kind of species mismatch this project has caught and corrected before).
- All 10 pins are approximate: park-, square- or garden-level addresses rather than a surveyed spot for any individual tree.
- Site rebuilt, all contracts validated, pushed.

Not used, same discipline as the Corstorphine mismatch already on record.

Every one of the 20 live cities has now had at least one genuine dedicated photo-hunting pass (see the string of 2026-07-22/23 entries below) and the last three runs (Berlin, Seville, Porto) already moved on to new cities rather than re-hunting the same gaps, so re-running identical searches again here would be the exact "looping forever" the mandate warns against. ## 2026-07-24 — Porto: 20th city live, 10 trees, 5 flagged, 8 photos missing

- New city, the next `pending` in the tier-1 list after Seville. Researched via the city council's own parks pages (ambiente.cm-porto.pt), its 2026 announcement of 21 newly classified "árvores de interesse público", Portuguese and English Wikipedia, and a dedicated local tree-history project (100milarvores.pt / FUTURO) that has documented individual specimens across Porto's historic gardens in detail.
- The throughline that fell out of the research: almost everything on this list traces to 19th-century British port wine families (Tait, among others) who filled their quintas with American, Asian and southern-hemisphere imports, plus the University of Porto's absorption of the former Campo Alegre estate into today's free public Botanical Garden. Two gardens, the Jardins do Palácio de Cristal and the Jardim Botânico do Porto, account for all ten trees. Both are free, confirmed directly rather than assumed.
- One correction caught mid-research, worth flagging: an official municipal press release listed a Himalayan cedar and other classified trees under "Palacete Burmester Garden", a University of Porto building (Casa dos Livros) whose current public-access policy for its own grounds could not be confirmed. Rather than guess, that tree and three others tied to the same address were dropped from consideration entirely, and the Himalayan cedar slot was filled instead with a specimen independently confirmed, by photograph and caption, to grow in the fully public Jardins do Palácio de Cristal.
- **5 trees flagged, all honestly, none fabricated**: the metrosidero and the Texas madrone both have confirmed locations and classification status but no age or planting date in any source found; the Himalayan cedar's "possibly largest in the city" claim is softened per hard rule 8, since more than one Porto garden makes a similar claim about a similarly-sized cedar and no citywide survey has settled it; the coast redwood is described only as "among the largest" with no individual measurement; the Atlas cedar rests on one single dedicated source, kept because the underlying facts check out even though the specific specimen isn't cross-confirmed.
- 2 of 10 photos found, both Wikimedia Commons, both opened and viewed directly before approval: a Himalayan cedar overlooking the city from the Palácio de Cristal gardens, and a camellia bloom for the Casa Tait hedge ensemble. **8 missing** after a genuine search across Commons for the rest; several close candidates (a generic tulip tree photo, a generic magnolia photo, a generic sequoia photo) were found and rejected because none were confirmed to be the actual Porto specimen rather than a stock photo of the species elsewhere.
- Site rebuilt, all contracts validated, pushed.

## 2026-07-24 — Seville: 19th city live, 10 trees, 4 flagged, 5 photos missing

- New city, the next `pending` in the tier-1 list after Berlin. Researched via Seville's official Inventario de Árboles Singulares (56 catalogued specimens plus 28 tree groves), Spanish Wikipedia, Spain's own "Árbol y Bosque del Año" historic-tree competition site, and several dedicated local botanical-history blogs (jardinesdelaoliva.wordpress.com, alcazarvegetal.wordpress.com, an atlas of the Real Alcázar's own plant collection).
- The throughline that fell out of the research: almost none of Seville's grandest trees are actually ancient. Most date to one of two deliberate planting booms, centuries of orange trees grown for blossom rather than fruit, and a wave of exotic imports (Australian figs and eucalyptus, Chinese ginkgos) planted around the 1910s-1920s and the 1929 Ibero-American Exposition. Used that as the city intro rather than force a false "ancient city" framing.
- One real correction mid-research, worth flagging clearly: the original lead candidate for tree #1 was a "500-to-600-year-old orange tree planted by King Pedro I" in the Real Alcázar gardens. On checking, the only source found was a single city-council blog post (now offline, only recoverable via search-engine cache) whose own figures disagreed with themselves (500 years in the headline, "over six centuries" in the body). Dropped for insufficient sourcing per hard rule 2 and replaced with a much better documented find in the same gardens: a grove of Ginkgo biloba planted in 1910 from La Granja de San Ildefonso, 10 of 25 originals still alive, confirmed via a dedicated botanical-history source plus an independent second article.
- **4 trees flagged, all honestly, none fabricated**: the Cartuja ombú's Hernando Colón/1529 origin story rests on one detailed source rather than two; the Glorieta de Bécquer's swamp cypress girth and age vary slightly between the two sources found; the María Luisa hackberries are a group entry without one definitive age; the Patio de los Naranjos' current orange trees are honestly dated to a 20th-century restoration rather than the Almohad-era mosque the courtyard was built for, a point most tourist material glosses over. Full detail in CURATION.md below.
- One single-sourced tree kept rather than dropped: the camphor tree of the Glorieta de Doña Sol, described as Seville's only known specimen by one dedicated local blog and not independently corroborated. Kept because the underlying facts (species rarity, general behaviour) are independently verifiable even if this specific tree isn't cross-confirmed; age left as unknown rather than guessed.
- 5 of 10 photos found, all Wikimedia Commons, all CC BY or CC BY-SA, every one fetched and viewed directly before approval (not just caption-matched): the Alcázar ginkgo grove, the Cartuja ombú, the Bécquer cypress, the Alameda de Hércules planes, and the Patio de los Naranjos. **5 missing** after a genuine search across Commons, iNaturalist and Flickr for each: the Cristo de Burgos ficus (several candidate files opened, none actually showed the plaza or its trees), the Gran Capitán eucalyptus (undocumented on Commons, consistent with the tree's own story of being locally obscure despite its size), the María Luisa hackberries, the Glorieta de Goya ficus, and the Doña Sol camphor tree.
- Access note: four of the ten trees stand inside sites that charge admission (Real Alcázar, the CAAC/Monasterio de la Cartuja, and Seville Cathedral's Patio de los Naranjos), all UNESCO-listed or nationally protected monuments. Noted honestly in each tree's access field rather than implied free.
- Site rebuilt, all contracts validated, pushed.

## 2026-07-24 — Berlin: 18th city live, 10 trees, 2 flagged, 3 photos missing

- New city, the next `pending` city after the whole photo-floor sweep across the 17 already-published cities wrapped up (each had a genuine hunting pass this cycle, recorded honestly; see the 2026-07-23 entries below). Researched via Berlin's official Naturdenkmal register (roughly 600 protected trees citywide, most recently overhauled 20 May 2021), the German Wikipedia's per-district Naturdenkmale lists and its "Liste markanter und alter Baumexemplare in Berlin" overview, plus local press (berliner-woche.de, Tagesspiegel) and district government pages for individual trees.
- **7/10 photos found**, all Wikimedia Commons, all CC BY-SA 3.0 or 4.0, licence and attribution recorded. Every photo was fetched and viewed directly this run, not just caption-matched, catching one near miss before it shipped: a file named for the Kaisereiche (the political-vandalism oak in Friedenau) actually showed a giant sequoia trunk when opened, a mismatch invisible from the filename or the search summary alone. Swapped for a second, visually-confirmed Kaisereiche photo instead (Berlin_Friedenau_Kaisereiche_22.06.2015, Dirk Ingo Franke, CC BY-SA 4.0). A separate Kaisereiche candidate (A.Savin, Free Art License) was found and rejected on licence grounds alone, since FAL isn't one of this project's three accepted licences. **3 missing** after a real search across Commons, iNaturalist and Flickr for each: the Steinlanke oak in Grunewald (too remote and obscure for dedicated photography), the Treptower Park elm (only park-level, uncredited images turned up), and the Britz ginkgo (a same-name mismatch found and rejected: a Commons file captioned for "Gutspark Britz" was actually a different ginkgo in Pankow once opened and checked against its own file page).
- **2 trees flagged, both honestly, neither fabricated**:
  - Dicke Marie, Berlin's oldest tree: a genuine, wide dispute between the city forestry office (800-900 years) and the German Dendrological Society's own 2021 National Heritage Tree assessment (500-600 years). Presented as a 500-900 range with the disagreement named directly in the story rather than picking the more dramatic number.
  - The Steinlanke oak in Grunewald: two credible sources give slightly different girth measurements and ages (6.48 m/350 years from a 2018 survey vs. a more commonly cited 6.70 m/400 years). Presented as a 350-400 range.
- One tree included with an honest "declining" label rather than dropped or prettied up: the Bellevue-Eiche in Köpenick, which local press has literally called a "sterbende Eiche" (dying oak) since 2015, when it was fenced off after a decade of visible failure. Kept because it is still alive and under active, well-documented conservation effort, and the story says plainly that it is not thriving.
- One access note worth flagging: the Treskow-Platane stands inside Tierpark Berlin's paid zoo grounds rather than an open park, an unusual condition for this list; included anyway since anyone can buy a ticket at the gate, with access marked "paid entry" rather than free.
- Species note: Berlin's own Naturdenkmal register is genuinely oak-heavy (pedunculate oak is the single most common protected species citywide), which is why 5 of the 10 trees here are oaks; used as the city's honest throughline rather than smoothed over for variety.
- Site rebuilt, all contracts validated on the first pass. Everything committed and pushed.

## 2026-07-23 — Prague: 10 trees, 4 flagged, 1 photo missing

- New city, the next `pending` in the tier-1 list after Vienna. Researched via Prague's official pamatny strom (memorial tree) register, which has protected individual trees since 1992 under national nature-conservation law and currently covers roughly 200 trees citywide, plus Czech Wikipedia's dedicated per-tree pages, prazskestromy.cz (the city's own memorial-tree walking-tour site), botany.cz and itras.cz.
- **9/10 photos found**, well above the 8/10 floor, all from Wikimedia Commons. Prague's memorial trees are individually and consistently photographed there (multiple contributors: Balaban, Yekaterina Borisova, Palickap, SJu, MartinVeselka, Sefjo, Limojoe), all CC BY-SA 3.0 or 4.0, licence and attribution recorded per tree, and every URL was independently verified to return HTTP 200 before use. **1 missing**: Neruda's Pear Tree (Seminarska zahrada), for which no open-licensed photo turned up anywhere searched (Commons, iNaturalist, Flickr); recorded honestly as missing rather than stretched.
- **4 trees flagged, all honestly, none fabricated**:
  - The Beethoven Plane (Velkoprevorske namesti): girth and protection date confirmed by two sources, but its age is genuinely disputed, ranging from a circa-1810 register estimate to a documented 1725-1727 planting window to one secondary source claiming 290 years. Stated as a wide 200-300 year range rather than false precision. The Beethoven-sat-here legend is explicitly debunked by both primary sources (he visited in February, an odd month for garden reveries).
  - The Plane of Karlovo namesti: girth and height confirmed by two sources, but age estimates disagree meaningfully (160-170 years per one source, approximately 190 per another). Stated as a 160-190 range.
  - The Ginkgo of Stromovka: girth and crown width confirmed, but two sources disagree by roughly 80 years on age (a circa-1804 planting estimate versus a 2016 assessment of about 130 years). Stated as a 140-220 range.
  - The Field Maple of the Seminary Garden: all measurements are internally consistent, but the only sources found are the Czech Wikipedia article and its linked Wikidata entry, effectively one underlying record rather than two independent ones, so flagged as thinner sourcing despite consistent data. Also the one tree on this city's list without exact source coordinates; pin set to approximate from the described location within the garden.
- **One tree dropped outright on hard rule 10**: Dub Karel in Kolodeje, Prague's actual oldest and most massive tree (550 years, 734 cm girth, a former "Tree of the Year"), sits in a private castle park explicitly reported as not publicly accessible. Left off the list entirely and named instead in the question-page context as the tree this site cannot send anyone to.
- **One candidate researched at length then dropped on the same rule**: an initial lead on a "400-year-old yew, the oldest tree at Prague Castle" turned out on closer checking to be two different trees conflated by a low-quality source. The actual old yew matching that description grows in the rajsky dvur, the enclosed cloister courtyard of the Franciscan monastery near Jungmannovo namesti (not Prague Castle at all), and a dedicated source states explicitly that public access to that specific courtyard is restricted, unlike the adjoining public Frantiskanska zahrada park. Dropped rather than published on a location this site could not confirm as open.
- **Another candidate dropped on the same rule**: the yews at the Turbova estate in Kosire (once Jiri Trnka's garden, the setting for his film The Garden), explicitly documented as not accessible to the public.
- **One correction mid-research**: a widely repeated claim that Stromovka holds "the oldest white oak of its species in the Czech Republic" turned up in multiple secondary sources but never with an age, girth or precise location for the specific tree, only the bare superlative. Per hard rule 8, dropped rather than published as an unverifiable record claim, and replaced on the list with the better-documented field maple in the Seminary Garden.
- One tree included with a clearly flagged access caveat rather than dropped: Neruda's Pear Tree is not a legally protected pamatny strom, only listed in Prague's separate, lower-tier "significant trees" inventory since 2013. Kept because it is genuinely old for its species (around 190 years, exceptional for a wild pear), publicly accessible, and well documented, with the status difference stated plainly in the story and notes rather than implied to be full legal protection.
- Species note: four London Planes (Platanus x acerifolia, the Czech "platan javorolisty") appear on this list, all genuinely distinct named specimens with their own protection orders and stories (a disputed-age palace tree, the city's most recognisable memorial tree, Petrin's largest tree, and one with a precisely documented 1813 planting date), kept separate rather than merged, matching how Vienna's list also carried three planes of its own.
- Site built and all contracts validated on first pass, no length fixes needed this time.


## 2026-07-23 — Vienna: 10 trees, 4 flagged, 0 photos missing

- New city, next `pending` in the tier-1 list after the lead group and marquee Europeans. Researched via Vienna's official Naturdenkmal (natural monument) register, which has protected individual trees since 1936 and gives an exact protection date for almost everything on the list, plus Wikipedia's district-by-district Naturdenkmäler lists, Wien Geschichte Wiki, and a handful of dedicated pages (Mozart-Platane, Stock im Eisen, Rathauspark, Volksgarten).
- **10/10 photos found**, matching London as the only other city to clear the floor outright, and on the first pass rather than a later hunt. Vienna's own heritage photographers (GuentherZ and Michael Kranewitter in particular) have systematically documented the city's Naturdenkmäler on Wikimedia Commons under consistent naming, which made this city unusually easy to photograph. All 10 are CC BY, CC BY-SA 3.0, CC BY-SA 3.0 AT or CC BY-SA 4.0, licence and attribution recorded per tree.
- **4 flagged**, all honestly, none fabricated:
  - The Sisi-Platane of Volksgarten (girth and Naturdenkmal number confirmed by two sources, but its "200 years old" age and the Empress Elisabeth nickname story only appear in secondary tourism material, not in either primary source).
  - The Oak of the Sulzwiese (girth 4.28 m and 45 m crown diameter confirmed by two sources, but no source anywhere gives an age; estimated 150-250 years from size alone rather than a record, stated as a wide range on purpose).
  - The Giant Sequoias of Pötzleinsdorfer Schlosspark (existence, species and location confirmed, but no individual measurement or planting date found for this specific pair; age bounded only by the fact that giant sequoias did not reach Europe before 1853).
  - The Twin Giants of Sternwartepark (a ginkgo and giant sequoia sharing one 2010 Naturdenkmal registration; the ginkgo's "~100 years" age is from that 2010 survey and has not been re-measured since).
- **One tree dropped outright on hard rule 10**: Vienna's actual oldest Naturdenkmal, a yew grove remnant at Rennweg 12 reputed to survive from Roman times, sits on the grounds of the European Patent Office. Multiple sources confirm it explicitly, "wächst nicht öffentlich zugänglich" (does not grow publicly accessible), and a visitor account describes being turned away twice. Left off the list entirely rather than published with a caveat, per "when in doubt, leave it out."
- **One candidate dropped on the same rule, more cautiously**: the 400-500 year old oak stand at Johannser Kogel in the Lainzer Tiergarten. The land itself is a public nature reserve, but the fenced core containing the old oaks is guided-tour-only by registration, explicitly to protect the trees from the same kind of visitor damage (soil compaction, trampling) that motivates rule 10 in the first place. Judged too close to "restricted to protect the tree" to publish as an open destination, even though it's technically public land.
- **One correction to a widely repeated claim**: initial research surfaced a "thickest tree in Vienna" black poplar in the Prater at 8.28 m girth. Cross-checking against Vienna's own Naturdenkmal list turned up a "former" (delisted) Naturdenkmal for a black poplar in the Prater at 5.7 m girth, and separately that the famous named specimen ("Der schwarze Turm") toppled in a storm in early 2015 and was chipped up on site. Rather than risk conflating a dead, delisted tree with an unverified living record-holder, dropped the black poplar entirely and used the well-documented Pedunculate Oak of the Sulzwiese instead.
- One dead relic included deliberately and labelled as such: Stock im Eisen, a nail-studded spruce trunk dated by dendrochronology to roughly 1400-1440, has not been a living tree for around 585 years. Included for its historic weight (Vienna's most recognisable tree-derived object, at Stephansplatz) but excluded from the "oldest tree in Vienna" answer, which goes to the oldest confirmed *living* tree on the list instead, mirroring how London's fallen Queen Elizabeth's Oak was handled.
- Species note: two Oriental Planes (Platanus orientalis) and one London Plane (Platanus x hispanica) both appear on this list; kept the historically-attested species per each tree's own dedicated source rather than merging them, since Vienna's planes genuinely split across both.
- Site built and all contracts validated on first pass after a `question_meta` length fix (160 to 139 chars).

## 2026-07-23 — Tokyo photo hunt: 7/10 to 8/10, second city to clear the floor

- Moved to the next tier per the ladder (Istanbul, Kyoto, New York, Tokyo) after closing out the marquee-European sweep. Tokyo was closest to the 8/10 floor at 7/10, needing just one more.
- Found and approved **tok_007** (the Ginkgo of the Sperm Discovery, Koishikawa Botanical Garden): a CC BY-SA 4.0 Commons photo, in the dedicated Commons category "The Ginkgo Where Sperm Was Discovered," whose own caption reads "a stele commemorating the 60th anniversary of the discovery of spermatozoids of Ginkgo biloba (1896-1956), next to the old specimen, more than 300 years old." Visually confirmed: the frame shows the memorial stone with a large tree trunk directly behind and beside it. A prior run rejected a different candidate for this exact tree because it showed only the marker with no tree visible; this one clears that bar since the trunk is genuinely in frame next to its own dedicated memorial.

- A methodology change worth flagging for future runs: this pass fetched several candidate images as raw binary and viewed them directly (the `Read` tool renders images fetched via `WebFetch`), rather than relying on captions and coordinates alone as prior passes have done ("this is a text-search workflow without image vision" appears repeatedly in earlier entries). That let this run visually confirm matches instead of leaving them as `found_needs_check`. Worth doing for other cities' unverified candidates too.
- Found and approved 5, all CC BY 4.0 from the Flanders Immovable Heritage Agency:
  - **ant_001** (Copper Beech of Sint-Willibrorduskerk): the agency's own designated photo for this exact protected tree (heritage object 130558, "Beuk, wassende voor de Sint-Willibrorduskerk"), same street match. Taken in winter with no leaves, so it doesn't show the tree's purple/copper foliage; noted honestly rather than implied.
  - **ant_002** (Summer Linden of Rivierenhof): a Wikimedia Commons copy of the same agency photo, visually confirmed as a large linden standing alone in grassland opposite a pavilion, matching the tree's own description. The photo's own coordinates (51.215238, 4.463411) sit about 480m from this entry's previous address-based estimate; updated the pin to the photo's coordinates since both cite the same heritage object (131461) and the photo's is the better-sourced figure. Left `location_precision` as `approximate`, since this still isn't a surveyed pin.
  - **ant_006** (Canadian Poplar of Eric Sasselaan): visually confirmed, a large poplar trunk next to brick fortification-wall structures, matching the tree's documented setting beside the Brialmont fortification remnant.
  - **ant_007** (Peace Tree of the Grote Markt): visually confirmed, a young linden in a bollard-and-chain enclosure directly in front of the "De Valk" building on the Grote Markt, matching this entry's own description exactly.
  - **ant_008** (Park Trees of Den Brandt, ensemble entry): visually confirmed, a formal garden view with clipped hedges, a bronze figure sculpture and the castle's dormered roofline in the background, filtered by the park's own heritage-object ID rather than guessed from a general search. Used as a representative ensemble shot, the same bar Brussels' pond and avenue ensembles were held to.
- Rejected on licence: three Flickr photos of Den Brandt (via the same "erfgoed" account) are CC BY-NC-SA, not usable for a commercial product; not used despite being an otherwise clean identity match.
- No usable photo found for the remaining 5 after genuine search across Commons, iNaturalist, Flickr, Openverse and the heritage image bank filtered by each tree's own heritage-object ID: ant_003 (Black Locust of Hertoghepark) and ant_004 (Black Mulberry of Hertoghepark), the one Hertoghepark photo found (image 244121) shows the park gate, not either named specimen; ant_005 (Cut-Leaved Horse Chestnut of Middelheim), the heritage-object-filtered results were generic castle-building photos, not tree-specific; ant_009 (Historic Grounds of Zoo Antwerpen), same issue, images were of buildings and irrigation infrastructure; ant_010 (Millennium Ginkgo of the Stadspark), no photo found anywhere, including Commons' own Stadspark category.

Checked the source directly: that "Stadspark" is in Tienen, not Antwerp, an unrelated park with the same common name. No conflict with the existing entry.
- Antwerp now at 5/10 approved. Below the 8/10 floor; recorded honestly.
